# Reference check 2: do the cited papers support the thesis claims?

> **Status (2026-10-15):** this report describes the thesis and `.bib` *before* the fixes. Tier A (bibliography) and tier B (wording) fixes have since been applied; see `fixes_applied.md` for every change and for the items left for tier C.

This was checked against the **full text of each paper**. The PDFs were downloaded to the *CAEM refs* Google Drive folder, converted to text and searched. Reference numbers are the printed thesis numbers.

For each reference, every place the thesis cites it is listed with the **full sentence** (or table row) carrying the citation, next to the **exact passage from the paper** that confirms or contradicts it, with its page.

**How the quotes were verified:** every quotation in the "What the paper says" column was machine-checked to occur word-for-word in the cited PDF's text (ignoring line breaks and hyphenation). Exceptions:
- quotes taken from a *suggested replacement* paper are labelled as such;
- table values are reported as numbers read from the named table;
- quotes from the scanned papers [31], [87], [92] and [93] match the OCR text at 96–99%; the differences are OCR errors only;
- text marked *Preliminary* comes from an abstract only, because the full PDF was not available.

**Verdicts**
- ✅ **Matched**: the paper says this. Citations that only attribute a method, model or dataset count as matched when the attribution is correct.
- ⚠️ **Partly**: part is right, but a number, detail or attribution is wrong or overstated (the *Why* column says what).
- ❌ **Not matched**: the paper does not say this, or says the opposite.
- 🔒 **PDF needed**: the PDF could not be obtained, so the claim is not yet checked.

**% matched** = ✅ ÷ citations checked against a PDF (🔒 rows excluded).

## Deeper passes (2026-10-15)

Three further passes were run after the first claim check:

1. **Cover-to-cover reading** of all 42 papers that had a ⚠️ or ❌ verdict, looking for any passage that would rescue or overturn the verdict. The result for each is shown as *Full-read result* under that reference.
2. **Caveat check** of the Limitations, Discussion and Conclusion sections of every paper that had only ✅ verdicts, looking for qualifications that conflict with how the thesis uses the paper. The result is shown as *Caveat check* under each reference.
3. **Follow-on sentences**: every uncited sentence that follows a citation in the same paragraph (297 sentences) was classified, and every one that makes a claim about a paper was checked against that paper. See Part 3 at the end.

### Verdicts changed by the deeper passes

| Citation | Before | After | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|
| [11] Ji et al., claim 40 ('structural property … not trained away') | ❌ | ⚠️ | Conclusion: 'Hallucination is an artifact of neural-based NLG'; LLMs 'still exhibit the hallucination problem' (§13). 'Cannot be trained away' is still unsupported. |
| [24] Qwen2.5, claim 106 ('CoT prompting is effective' at 3B) | ✅ | ⚠️ | The report gives no evidence of CoT gains at 3B; the thesis's own B2 < B1 contradicts it. |
| [37] Niculescu-Mizil & [38] Geifman, chapter 4 line 348 (fixed threshold 'robust to the cal-versus-eval shift') | ✅ | ⚠️ | Both papers assume calibration and test data come from the same distribution (SGR Thm 3.2: 'sampled i.i.d. from P'), so neither supports shift robustness. |
| [58] rsLoRA, claim 198 (α = 2r 'standard scaling … avoids gradient collapse') | ✅ | ⚠️ | α = 2r keeps α/r constant, so collapse cannot occur, and it is not LoRA's standard scaling; by Thm 3.2 a constant scale is on the *unstable* side for large r. |
| [20] Wang (s_avg), [42] INSIDE, [16] EWC, [94] Ovadia | ⚠️/❌ | unchanged | Stronger quotes found and notes refined (see each reference). |

### New findings from the deeper passes that bear on the design (for tier C)

| # | Source | Finding | Effect on CAEM |
|---|---|---|---|
| 9 | [65] Song et al. §5 | 'iterative self-improvement typically saturates after two or three rounds, regardless of the model's capacity'; 'Small Models can not Self-improve' with self-verification (0.5B Qwen); the first-round gain at 7B/14B came from 'improved adherence to the required answer format', not self-improvement | CAEM runs 5 cycles at 3B and scores by exact match. Report a flexible-match metric next to EM to rule out format learning; frame the verifier as *external* cross-verification (where Song finds the gap grows with verifier capability). |
| 10 | [90] ReST §6; [66] Yi et al. | 'one Grow step is the best option when considering human evaluation scores, even though rewards continue to grow … risk of overfitting to the reward model increases with the repeated iterations'; gains 'plateau or deteriorate as verifier bias accumulates' | The verifier plays the reward model's role. State that held-out EM, not verifier score, is the success criterion, and discuss over-optimisation to the verifier. |
| 11 | [37] Niculescu-Mizil & Caruana §5 | Isotonic regression overfits below about 200–1000 calibration points; Platt is better there | CAEM fits per-signal isotonic curves on 500 samples per benchmark (admission floor 50). Cite this as the reason for the shrinkage prior, or acknowledge it. |
| 12 | [61] STaR §7 | 'settings with a high level of chance performance (e.g. binary decisions) yield many poor rationales' | StrategyQA is yes/no: verifier-accepted answers can be right for the wrong reason. Add to threats to validity. |
| 13 | [56] Biderman et al. | 'LoRA substantially underperforms full finetuning'; 'forgetting tends to worsen with training duration' | Expect small per-cycle gains and accumulating drift; cite both sides of this paper. |
| 14 | [16] EWC p.4 | Plain L2 (= uniform-Fisher EWC) is the *failing* baseline in the EWC paper | Re-word the L2-anchor description (the anchor is inactive, so results are unaffected). |
| 15 | [78] Sinha §Discussion | 'Stop using Cosine Similarity for Safety' for RLHF-era hallucinations | s_avg is a cosine-agreement signal. The setting differs (sample agreement, not answer-vs-source), but acknowledge it. |
| 16 | [19] Wei §3.1 | Wei's GPT-3 models are InstructGPT (instruction-tuned) and still show the ~100B CoT threshold | Removes the basis for 'the threshold does not apply to instruction-tuned models' (tier-C #4 becomes stronger). |
| 17 | [60] Natarajan | Guarantees require noise-rate-aware loss correction (Thm 3/11); plain training recovers the clean classifier only under symmetric noise | Anchor α > ½ on ρ₊ + ρ₋ < 1 as a learnability *condition*, not as a transferred guarantee (refines tier-C #1). |
| 18 | [66] Yi et al. App. B | Convergence proof uses 'concentration bounds and supermartingale inequalities' | A better citation (with Robbins–Siegmund 1971) for the thesis's supermartingale sentence than Robbins–Monro. |
| 19 | [80] Valentin et al. fn. 3 | Logistic regression on score logits; XGBoost and Random Forest gave no gain | Directly supports CAEM's boost-layer choice (strengthens tier-C #5). |

## [2] Harnessing the Power of LLMs in Practice: A Survey on ChatGPT and Beyond

## [3] TruthfulQA: Measuring How Models Mimic Human Falsehoods
`lin-etal-2022-truthfulqa` · Lin et al., 2022 · paper found: ✅ Yes · details matched: ✅ Yes

**Full-read result (confirmed):** Full read of the main text and Appendices A–F. Table 4 (App. B.2) gives %True+info for GPT-3-175B as: QA 18.2, null 23.4, chat 23.3, long-form 24.0, helpful 21.4, harmful 10.9. The best is 24.0%; no figure of 25% appears anywhere. The human baseline is 94% true and 87% true+informative (§4.1). App. B.3 gives an MC-task human baseline of 95%; no 94% figure exists for true+informative. Verdicts stand: the thesis's 25% should be 21% (helpful prompt, the headline number) and its 94% should be 87% for true+informative.

**Cited 9 times. Matched 7 of 9 checked = 78%** (partly 2 · not matched 0 · PDF needed 0)

## [4] Enhanced Conformal Prediction with Boosted Logistic Regression
`cherian2024conformal` · Cherian et al., 2024 · paper found: ✅ Yes · details matched: ❌ No

**Full-read result (confirmed):** Full read (main text, §3.3 conditional boosting, §4.2–4.3 experiments, appendices searched). The word 'logistic' never appears. Boosting is: 'we boost our claim score … by optimizing a linear combination of four scores' (p.10), with θ learned by gradient ascent on claim retention, 'differentiating through the conditional conformal procedure' (abstract); the indicator is smoothed by a sigmoid as in Stutz et al. [Ref 34] is the right precedent for logistic aggregation.

**Full-read result (confirmed):** Full read. The bib key huang-etal-2023-large is Jiaxin Huang et al., 'Large Language Models Can Self-Improve' (EMNLP 2023). It fine-tunes PaLM-540B on its own high-confidence self-consistency answers (GSM8K 74.4→82.1). It never discusses self-correction from external feedback and is not a hallucination survey. All 3 ❌ verdicts stand. The 'rarely improves without external feedback' claim is Jie Huang et al. ICLR 2024 ('LLMs struggle to self-correct their responses without external feedback', p.1). Side note: LMSI itself is a close precedent for CAEM's confidence-filtered self-training and could be cited for that instead.

## [16] Overcoming catastrophic forgetting in neural networks
`doi:10.1073/pnas.1611835114` · Kirkpatrick et al., 2017 · paper found: ✅ Yes · details matched: ✅ Yes

**Full-read result (confirmed+new caveat):** Full read (main text, discussion, appendices). The text gives no '≈90%' or any percentage figure; results appear only as plots (Figs 2–3). The ❌ verdict stands. New caveat that affects the thesis's design text: the paper uses plain L2 (a 'fixed quadratic constraint for each weight', which is exactly the uniform-Fisher case) as a *failing* baseline. 'task B cannot be learned properly as the constraint protects all weights equally, leaving little spare capacity for learning on B' (p.4, Fig 2A). So the thesis's 'explicit L2 anchor that approximates EWC under a uniform-Fisher assumption' is mathematically true, but the EWC paper presents that approximation as the thing EWC fixes. The anchor is inactive in CAEM, so there is no effect on results; reword so it does not imply EWC endorses L2.

## [17] Detecting Hallucinations in Large Language Models Using Semantic Entropy
`farquhar2024semantic` · Farquhar et al., 2024 · paper found: ✅ Yes · details matched: ✅ Yes

**Full-read result (confirmed):** Full read (main text, Discussion, Methods). Semantic entropy targets confabulations, 'claims that are both wrong and arbitrary', and is distinguished 'from … when LLMs are consistently wrong as a result of being trained on erroneous data such as common misconceptions' (p.625). The Discussion repeats: 'Our method explicitly does not directly address situations in which LLMs are confidently wrong' (p.630). Clustering is by bidirectional entailment (DeBERTa-large-MNLI or LLM prompt), with 'ten generations' at 'temperature 1'. The only learned combiner in the paper is the embedding-regression *baseline*, a logistic regression on hidden states; there is no weighted-sum ensemble. The paper never discusses accumulating verified knowledge. All verdicts stand.

## [26] MiniCheck: Efficient Fact-Checking of LLMs on Grounding Documents
`tang2024minicheck` · Tang et al., 2024 · paper found: ✅ Yes · details matched: ⚠️ Partly

**Full-read result (confirmed+new caveat):** Full read (main text, limitations, appendices searched). The paper reports balanced accuracy at a fixed 0.5 threshold and never claims calibrated probabilities. It adds: 'In practice, most fact-checkers return scores at the extremes of the range, so small tweaks on this procedure have little effect' (§5, p.8822). That is evidence *against* reading MiniCheck's output as a calibrated probability, and it supports CAEM's per-signal isotonic recalibration step. MiniCheck-FT5 is flan-t5-large (770M) trained on 21K ANLI + 14K synthetic (§4). The ⚠️ verdict stands; reword as 'returns a support probability that CAEM recalibrates'.

## [37] Predicting Good Probabilities With Supervised Learning
`niculescu2005predicting` · Niculescu-Mizil & Caruana, 2005 · paper found: ✅ Yes · details matched: ✅ Yes

**Full-read result (confirmed+new design caveat):** Full read (Niculescu-Mizil & Caruana, ICML 2005). Isotonic regression fits 'an isotonic (monotonically increasing) function' by PAV (§2.2). There is no direction auto-detection and no exchangeability bound, so both ⚠️ verdicts stand. NEW, design-relevant: §5 learning curves: 'When the calibration set is small (less than about 200-1000 cases), Platt Scaling outperforms Isotonic Regression with all nine learning methods … Isotonic Regression is less constrained … easier for it to overfit'; isotonic is as good or better only 'When there are 1000 or more points in the calibration set'. CAEM fits per-signal isotonic curves on a 500-sample calibration fold per benchmark, which is inside the range where this paper finds Platt better. The James-Stein shrinkage toward the pooled fit partly mitigates this. Add one sentence acknowledging it (or cite this as the reason for shrinkage). Proposed as tier-C minor item.

## [56] LoRA Learns Less and Forgets Less
`biderman2024lora` · Biderman et al., 2024 · paper found: ✅ Yes · details matched: ✅ Yes

**Full-read result (confirmed+new caveat):** Full read (TMLR 08/2024, incl. Discussion, Conclusion and the S-tables cited in §4.2). The ⚠️ verdict stands: numbers are average source-domain accuracies (e.g. code IFT, full FT 0.414 vs LoRA r=64 0.509, Table S6), and they degrade with epochs. NEW caveats relevant to CAEM's iterative LoRA cycles: (a) title and abstract: 'LoRA substantially underperforms full finetuning' on the target domain ('LoRA Learns Less'); (b) 'forgetting tends to worsen with training duration' (§4.2); (c) in math, 'LoRA with r = 256 forgets nearly as much as full finetuning'. So the paper supports 'LoRA forgets less than full FT', but it also predicts that per-cycle gains will be small and that drift accumulates over cycles. Cite both sides.

**Cited 2 times. Matched 1 of 2 checked = 50%** (partly 1 · not matched 0 · PDF needed 0)

| # | Where | Full cited text in the thesis | What the paper says (exact quote) | Page | Verdict | Why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `chapters/chapter_2.tex:212` | Biderman and colleagues sweep Llama-2-7B across rank settings from sixteen to two hundred and fifty-six on Magicoder coding instruction data \cite{biderman2024lora} and report two findings that translate directly to CAEM's setting: low-rank adaptation retains roughly sixty-six percent of HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, and Winogrande accuracy at every rank tested, against sixty-two percent retention for full fine-tuning matched on epoch count, and the low-rank versus full gap on retention dominates the rank-versus-rank gap on fit. | Table S6 "Magicoder-Evol-Instruct-110K Results (Forgetting Average)" (avg of HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande), epochs 1/2/4/8/16 — LoRA r=16: 0.653/0.648/0.652/0.646/0.609; r=64: 0.652/0.651/0.632/0.580/0.510; r=256: 0.655/0.659/0.631/0.552/0.517; Full FT: 0.595/0.579/0.512/0.446/0.414. "three LoRA models with ranks r = 16, 64, 256" | Table S6 (Appendix D); §3 | ⚠️ Partly | Llama-2-7B, Magicoder and ranks 16–256 are correct. But the numbers are **average accuracies**, not "retention percentages". LoRA is about 0.65 only at early epochs: r=64/256 fall to about 0.51–0.52 by epoch 16, so "at every rank tested" is false. ## [58] A Rank Stabilization Scaling Factor for Fine-Tuning with LoRA
`kalajdzievski2023rslora` · Kalajdzievski, 2023 · paper found: ✅ Yes · details matched: ✅ Yes

**Caveat check (verdict changed on [198]):** Conclusion: rsLoRA 'remains stable and non-collapsing even for very large adapter ranks'. Re-checking claim 198 against Thm 3.2 showed that the thesis's 'standard α = 2r scaling … avoids the gradient collapse' reasoning is muddled (α = 2r is not LoRA's standard scaling, and a constant scale cannot collapse). Downgraded ✅→⚠️ with suggested wording.

## [60] Learning with Noisy Labels
`natarajan2013learning` · Natarajan et al., 2013 · paper found: ✅ Yes · details matched: ✅ Yes

**Full-read result (confirmed+refines tier C #1):** Full read (NeurIPS 2013). Setting: class-conditional noise with 'ρ+1 + ρ−1 < 1' and the rates 'assumed … known to the learner' (§2; tuned by CV in experiments). Guarantees come only from *modified* losses: the unbiased estimator (Thm 3) or the label-dependent weighted loss (Thm 11). Lemma 7: under asymmetric noise the noisy Bayes classifier thresholds η(x) at a shifted point, and plain training recovers the clean classifier only when ρ+1 = ρ−1 (α* = 1/2, p.6). The ⚠️ verdict stands. For tier-C fix #1: anchor α>½ on ρ++ρ−<1 as the *learnability* condition, but state that the paper's guarantee needs noise-rate-aware loss correction, which CAEM does not apply. So the link is an analogy plus a condition, not a transferred theorem.

There is no learned verifier, so the ⚠️ verdict stands. CAEM self-trains on StrategyQA (yes/no), where a 50% chance floor means a verifier-accepted answer can be right for the wrong reason. Worth one sentence in the limitations / threats-to-validity section.

**Full-read result (confirmed+NEW DESIGN THREAT):** Full read (arXiv 2412.02674v2). There is no 'if and only if', no formal sufficient condition and no 'noisy-label-recovery task'; it is a controlled empirical study. The ⚠️ verdicts stand. NEW findings that bear directly on CAEM: (1) 'Without new information, iterative self-improvement typically saturates after two or three rounds, regardless of the model's capacity' (§5 takeaway); CAEM reports 5 cycles. (2) 'Small Models can not Self-improve … gap(f) is non-positive for nearly all verification methods' for Qwen-1.5 0.5B, Qwen-2 0.5B and Llama-2 7B (§4.2). CAEM's backbone is Qwen2.5-3B, though its verifier is mostly external (MiniCheck etc.), which is the 'cross-verification' case where 'the GV-gap increases with verifier capability' (§1). (3) The first-round accuracy jump for 7B/14B 'is attributed to improved adherence to the required answer format … the discrepancy between "flexible match" and "exact match" … disappears after the first round … not due to the self-improvement capability' (§5). CAEM scores with exact match, so a cycle-1 gain may be format learning. Recommend: report flexible-match alongside EM, and frame CAEM's verifier as external cross-verification.

## [78] Semantic Illusion: Generic Natural Language Inference Models Miscalibrate on Language Model Hallucinations → checked against the real paper: Sinha 2025, *The Semantic Illusion* (arXiv:2512.15068)
`semanticillusion2025` · Anon., 2025 · paper found: ❌ No · details matched: ❌ No

**Caveat check (no conflict + design caveat):** Discussion/Future Work: 'modern hallucinations … are semantically indistinguishable from truth using vector similarity or standard NLI'; '1. Stop using Cosine Similarity for Safety … Any production system relying solely on embedding similarity for hallucination detection should be considered uncalibrated'; 'Our calibration assumes exchangeability … distribution shift is inevitable as models are updated'. The citation for claim 247 (generic NLI fails on HaluEval) is supported. Caveat: CAEM's s_avg is a mean pairwise *cosine similarity* in sentence-embedding space. Sinha's setting is RAG faithfulness (answer vs. context), not agreement across samples, so the warning does not directly apply, but the thesis should note it. Also, 'as models are updated' is exactly CAEM's per-cycle LoRA update (tier-C #6).

## [90] Reinforced Self-Training (ReST) for Language Modeling
`gulcehre2023rest` · Gulcehre et al., 2023 · paper found: ✅ Yes · details matched: ✅ Yes

**Caveat check (no conflict + design caveat):** §6 Discussion: 'improvements in reward model scores do not necessarily reflect human preferences … one Grow step is the best option when considering human evaluation scores, even though rewards continue to grow with more Grow steps … the risk of overfitting to the reward model increases with the repeated iterations of the Grow steps'. ReST also uses 'progressively increasing filtering thresholds in the Improve step'. For CAEM, the verifier plays the reward model's role across 5 cycles, so this is the same over-optimisation risk that Song [65] and Yi [66] point to. Evaluating on held-out EM (not verifier score) is the right guard, and the thesis should say so explicitly.

# Part 3: Follow-on sentences

Every uncited sentence that follows a citation in the same paragraph: **297 sentences**. Each is listed, including those that are only about CAEM. Sentences inside tables are excluded. The 'Preceding citation' column gives the most recent citation in the paragraph.

**Classes**
- **own**: CAEM's own design, result, argument or a transition. It makes no claim about a paper, so nothing to check.
- **paper✓ / general✓**: a claim about a paper (or common background) that the paper supports. The quote and source are given.
- **paper⚠️ / paper❌ / contradicted**: a claim about a paper that is partly wrong, or wrong.
- **needs-cite**: a factual claim with no source behind it.
- **illustrative / not from paper**: an example presented as if from the paper but not in it.
- **wording**: grammar or fragment problems only.

**Totals:** own 172 · supported 81 · needs action 44

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| F210 | `chapters/chapter_2.tex:303` | CAEM extends this tradition in three specific ways: the memory is populated only through a four-outcome storage decision governed by a fixed-threshold rule on the calibrated probability composite, with a bounded deferred-reconsider buffer (capacity $K_{\text{def}}$, time-to-live $\tau_{\text{ttl}}$) for borderline items rather than from every retrieval; routing combines similarity with stored-verifier confidence rather than using similarity alone; and an independent safety override forces Tier~3 grounding whenever pre-routing confidence falls below the registered safety floor, regardless of the best memory match. | graves2014neural, weston2015memory, graves2016differentiable, NEURIPS2020_6b493230, 8733051, reimers-gurevych-2019-sentence | own |  |  | CAEM's own design, result, or transition; no claim about a cited paper. |
| F211 | `chapters/chapter_2.tex:305` | Together, these pieces motivate CAEM's cycle-boundary mechanics: retroactive re-verification of stored items against the refreshed adapter, deferred-buffer reconsideration with a three-way branch (promote, drop, keep), pool reweighting with a temperature softmax and a hard share cap, parameter-efficient fine-tuning on the high-purity training pool through the bounded LoRA adapter envelope, and a multi-modal retention guard with floor $\rho_{\min} = 0.93$ on the worst-probe ratio that aborts the cycle and rolls back the adapter (but not the memory) when general capability erodes. | huang-etal-2023-large, kamoi-etal-2024-llms | own |  |  | CAEM's own design, result, or transition; no claim about a cited paper. |
